\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&1&-2&1&0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&1&-1&-1&1&0&\cdots&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&1&-1&0&-1&1&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&1&-1&0&\cdots&\cdots\cdots&0&-1&1\end{pmatrix}.

Equivalently, if
i<s-1
or
i>s
, the
i
th column
C_{is}
of
M(s)
has entries
1,-1
in rows
i,i+1
(resp.
i-1,i
); if
i=s-1
(resp.
i=s
), entry
2
(resp.
-2
) in row
s-1
(resp.
s
) and
1
(resp.
-1
) elsewhere. Let us show that
M(s)
is invertible. It is straightforward to check that the row vector

c_{is}:=(\overbrace{-k+i,\ldots,-k+i}^{i-1},m,\overbrace{i,\ldots,i}^{k-i-1}),m:=-k+i\text{ if }i<s\text{ and }m:=i\text{ if }i\geq s

satisfies
c_{is}\cdot C_{js}=-k\delta_{ij}
, whence the matrix with rows
c_{1s},\ldots,c_{k-1s}
scaled by
-1/k
is the inverse of
M(s)
. Therefore
\varphi_{h_{i}e_{rs}}=-\frac{1}{k}c_{is}\cdot V^{rs}
.

In the extreme cases,
s=1
and
s=k
, the matrix
M(s)
follows the same pattern with the obvious changes and the same formula gives the inverse.

(3)

For
\varphi_{h_{i}h_{j}}
check that, for
r\neq j,j+1
,

E_{jr}E_{rj}E_{j+1j}E_{jj+1}=\varphi_{e_{rj+1}e_{rj+1}}+\varphi_{h_{r-1}h_{j}}-\varphi_{h_{r}h_{j}}-\varphi_{h_{j-1}h_{j}}+\varphi_{h_{j}h_{j}}

by showing first that

E_{rj}E_{j+1j}=-\varphi_{e_{jj+1}e_{rj}}-\varphi_{e_{jr}e_{j+1j}}

and

(E_{rj}E_{j+1j})E_{jj+1}=\varphi_{e_{jj+1}e_{rj+1}}-\varphi_{e_{jr}h_{j}}.

Next apply that
\varphi_{e_{rj+1}e_{rj+1}}=\frac{1}{4}E_{j+1r}^{2}E_{rj+1}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}E_{j+1r}^{2}E_{rj+1}^{2}E_{j+1j}E_{jj+1}
to find

W^{jr}:=E_{rj}E_{j+1j}E_{jj+1}-\frac{1}{4}E_{j+1r}^{2}E_{rj+1}^{2}E_{j+1j}E_{jj+1}=\varphi_{h_{r-1}h_{j}}-\varphi_{h_{r}h_{j}}-\varphi_{h_{j-1}h_{j}}+\varphi_{h_{j}h_{j}}\text{ for }r\neq j,j+1.

Check also that

W^{jj}:=E_{jj+1}E_{j+1j}^{2}E_{jj+1}=2(-\varphi_{h_{j-1}h_{j}}+2\varphi_{h_{j}h_{j}}-\varphi_{h_{j+1}h_{j}}).

Now fix
j
and proceed as in the previous case by solving for
\varphi^{j}:=(\varphi_{h_{1}h_{j}},\ldots,\varphi_{h_{k-1}h_{j}})
the
(k-1)\times(k-1)
system of linear equations
W^{j}=-M(j)\varphi^{j}
generated by (
3
) and (
3
), where
W^{j}:=(W^{j1},\ldots,W^{jj-1},\frac{1}{2}W^{jj},W^{jj+2},\ldots,W^{jk})

and
M(s)
is (thankfully!) the matrix described in the previous item. Therefore
\varphi_{h_{i}h_{j}}=\frac{1}{k}c_{ij}\cdot W^{j}
.

(4)

For
\varphi_{gg}
use that
1_{U^{\op}}=\varphi_{gg}+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{S}}\varphi_{aa}
.∎

​​ 3.4.3
.

Generator of
\ker\phi
.

The ideal
\ker\phi
of
U(L)^{\op}
is infinite dimensional, but is finitely generated; in this subsection, we show, through the primitive element lemma, that it is in fact a principal ideal. For most of this section we work with
U(L)
.

Let
\Ad_{k}
denote the adjoint representation of
\SL
and
\chi_{k}
its associated central character, and let
\phi_{k}
be the representation of
\SL
on
M_{k}(F)
given by the action of
\ad
. We have
\phi_{k}=\Ad_{k}\oplus\rho_{0}
with
\rho_{0}
acting on
F\cdot g
. Fixing the Cartan subalgebra of diagonal traceless matrices and the set of positive roots giving
N_{+}=\spn\{e_{12},e_{23},\ldots,e_{k-1k}\}
, the highest weight vector of
\Ad_{k}
is
e_{1k}
.

Denote
x^{i}_{j}:=e_{ij}
for
1\leq i\neq j\leq k
, and for
1\leq i\leq k
,

x^{i}_{i}:=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\alpha_{ij}h_{j},\,\,\alpha_{ij}:=k-j\text{ if }j\geq i,\,\,\alpha_{ij}:=-j\text{ if }j<i.

The elements
x^{i}_{j}
form a set of generators of
\SL
satisfying
[x^{i}_{j},x^{r}_{s}]=\delta_{jr}x^{i}_{s}-\delta_{is}x^{r}_{j}
. Then the Casimir elements

c_{p,k}:=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}=1}^{k}x^{i_{1}}_{i_{2}}x^{i_{2}}_{i_{3}}\cdots x^{i_{p}}_{i_{1}},\,\,2\leq p\leq k,

which have rational coefficients in the PBW basis, form a set of Casimir generators of
Z(U(L))
(see
[
35
, (6),(64)]
). For example,

\displaystyle c_{3,3}

\displaystyle=(x^{1}_{1})^{3}+(x^{1}_{1})^{2}x^{1}_{2}+(x^{1}_{1})^{2}x^{1}_{3}+x^{1}_{1}x^{1}_{2}x^{2}_{2}+x^{1}_{1}x^{1}_{2}x^{2}_{3}+\cdots+(x^{3}_{3})^{3}=

\displaystyle=2/9h_{1}^{3}+1/3h_{1}^{2}h_{2}-1/3h_{1}h_{2}^{2}-2/9h_{2}^{3}+2h_{1}^{2}+h_{1}h_{2}+4h_{1}+2h_{2}+

\displaystyle+h_{1}e_{21}e_{12}+2h_{2}e_{21}e_{12}-2h_{1}e_{32}e_{23}-h_{2}e_{32}e_{23}+3e_{31}e_{12}e_{23}+3e_{21}e_{32}e_{13}+h_{1}e_{31}e_{13}-h_{2}e_{31}e_{13}+

\displaystyle+6e_{21}e_{12}+3e_{31}e_{13}.

Let
\lambda_{p,k}
denote the eigenvalue of
c_{p,k}
for
\Ad_{k}
. These eigenvalues are the following positive integers.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Put
\lambda_{1,k}:=0
and
\lambda_{p,k}:=\chi_{k}(c_{p,k})
for
2\leq p\leq k
. Then
\lambda_{p,k}=\lambda_{p-2,k}+k^{p-1}
with
\lambda_{2,k}=2k
, thus

\lambda_{p,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\displaystyle k\left(\frac{k^{p}-1}{k^{2}-1}+1\right),&p\text{ even}\\
\displaystyle k^{2}\frac{k^{p-1}-1}{k^{2}-1},&p\text{ odd}\end{array}\right..

Proof.

By
[
35
, (8),(14),(16)]
we have
\lambda_{p,k}=\tr(A_{k}^{p}E_{k})
, where
E_{k}
is the
k\times k
matrix full of ones and
A_{k}
is the
k\times k
upper triangular matrix with
(i,j)
entries equal to
-1
when
i<j
and diagonal

(m_{1}+k-1,m_{2}+k-2,\ldots,m_{k-1}+1,m_{k}),

where
m_{i}
is the eigenvalue of
x_{i}^{i}
for the highest weight vector of the adjoint representation, i.e.,
x_{i}^{i}e_{1k}=:m_{i}e_{1k}
. A straightforward computation with Formula (
3.4.3
) produces
m_{1}=1
,
m_{2}=\cdots=m_{k-1}=0
,
m_{k}=-1
. By induction on
p
with base case
p=1
it is proven that
A_{k}^{p}E_{k}=A_{k}\cdot(A_{k}^{p-1}E_{k})
equals

\begin{pmatrix}a_{p,k}&a_{p,k}&\cdots&a_{p,k}\\
0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1&-1&\cdots&-1\end{pmatrix}

when
p
is odd and

\begin{pmatrix}a_{p,k}&a_{p,k}&\cdots&a_{p,k}\\
1&1&\cdots&1\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&1&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix}

when
p
is even, with
a_{1,k}=1
,

a_{p,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}ka_{p-1,k}+1,&p\text{ even}\\
ka_{p-1,k}-(k-1),&p>1\text{ odd}\end{array}\right.,

and

\lambda_{p,k}=\tr(A^{p}_{k}E_{k})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}a_{p,k}+k-1,&p\text{ even}\\
a_{p,k}-1,&p>1\text{ odd}\end{array}\right..

Therefore

\lambda_{p,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}k(a_{p-1,k}+1)=k(\lambda_{p-1,k}+2),&p\text{ even}\\
k(a_{p,k}-1)=k(\lambda_{p-1,k}-k),&p>1\text{ odd}\end{array}\right.,

with
\lambda_{2,k}=2k
,
\lambda_{1,k}=0
. Notice that
\lambda_{p,k}-\lambda_{p-2,k}=k(\lambda_{p-1,k}-\lambda_{p-3,k})
regardless of whether
p
is even or odd. By recursion
\lambda_{p,k}-\lambda_{p-2,k}=k^{p-3}(\lambda_{3,k}-\lambda_{1,k})=k^{p-3}k^{2}=k^{p-1}
, hence by recursion again we find

\lambda_{p,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p/2-1}k^{2i+1}+2k=k\left(\frac{k^{p}-1}{k^{2}-1}+1\right),&p\text{ even}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{(p-1)/2}k^{2i}=k^{2}\frac{k^{p-1}-1}{k^{2}-1},&p\text{ odd}\end{array}\right..\qed

Clearly
c_{p,k}-\lambda_{p,k}\in\ker\chi_{k}\subseteq\ker\Ad_{k}
for
2\leq p\leq k
, and we also have
e_{ij}^{3}\in\ker\Ad_{k}
for
1\leq i\neq j\leq k
. We prove that these elements generate
\ker\Ad_{k}
in
U(L)
and show that
\ker\phi_{k}
is a principal ideal. We build on ideas from
[
6
, Corollary 3.1.4 and Proposition 3.1.7]
, which solve the problem for
k=3
.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Denote
z_{p,k}:=c_{p,k}-\lambda_{p,k}
for
2\leq p\leq k
and
z^{\prime}_{p,k}:=\lambda_{2,k}c_{p,k}-\lambda_{p,k}c_{2,k}
for
3\leq p\leq k
.

(1)

\ker\Ad_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3},z_{2,k},\ldots,z_{k,k}\rangle
.

(2)

\ker\phi_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3},e_{12}z_{2k},z^{\prime}_{3,k},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{p,k}\rangle
.

(3)

\ker\phi_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3}+e_{12}z_{2,k}+e_{13}z_{3,k}+\cdots+e_{1k}z_{k,k}\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
K
be a field extension of
F
. Then
\Sl_{k}(K)=\SL\otimes_{F}K
,
U(\Sl_{k}(K))=U(L)\otimes_{F}K
, the adjoint representation of
\Sl_{k}(K)
is
\Ad_{k}\otimes_{F}K
, and if
\rho
is a representation of
\SL
then
\rho\otimes_{F}K
is a representation of
\Sl_{k}(K)
such that

\ker_{U(\Sl_{k}(K))}(\rho\otimes_{F}K)=(\ker_{U(L)}\rho)\otimes_{F}K.

In addition, if
I
is an ideal of
U(L)
such that
I\otimes_{F}K=\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
in
U(\Sl_{k}(K))
with
g_{i}\in I
for
1\leq i\leq n
then
I=\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
in
U(L)
. Therefore, by extension and restriction of scalars, we can assume without loss of generality that
F=\mathds{C}
.

(1)

Clearly
I_{k}:=\langle e_{12}^{3},z_{2,k},\ldots,z_{k,k}\rangle\subseteq\ker\Ad_{k}
. Let us show the opposite inclusion. First, we see that
I_{k}
has finite codimension. Consider a deglex order on the set of monomials of
U(L)
with
h_{i}>e_{ii+1}>e_{i+1i}
for
1\leq i<k
. Since
U(L)=I_{k}\oplus\spn N(I_{k})
, the ideal
I_{k}
has finite codimension if and only if
\spn N(I_{k})
has finite dimension, hence if and only if there are
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k^{2}-1},m_{1},\ldots,m_{k-1}\in\N
such that
e_{ij}^{n_{ij}},h_{l}^{m_{l}}\in\LM(I_{k})
for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq k
and
1\leq l<k
. In the next identities let
\ad
denote the adjoint map of
U(L)
; since we have

\displaystyle e_{21}^{3}=-\frac{1}{6!}\ad_{e_{21}}^{6}(e_{12}^{3})\text{ if }k=2,

\displaystyle e_{ij}^{3}=\frac{1}{6}\ad_{e_{il}}^{3}(e_{lj}^{3}),\,\,\,\,e_{ij}^{3}=-\frac{1}{6}\ad_{e_{lj}}^{3}(e_{il}^{3})\,\,\text{ for }i\neq l\neq j\neq i\text{ if }k>2,

starting from
e_{12}^{3}
we can show
e_{ij}^{3}\in I_{k}
for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq k
, for all
k\geq 2
: for
k\geq 3
, use the third identity to get
e_{1j}^{3}
for all
3\leq j\leq k
from
e_{12}^{3}
, the second identity to get
e_{i2}^{3}
for all
3\leq i\leq k
from
e_{12}^{3}
, then the second identity again to get
e_{21}^{3}
from
e_{31}^{3}
; and so on. Also, since for all
k\geq 2
and all
1\leq i<k
we have

\frac{1}{6}\ad^{3}_{e_{ii+1}}(e_{i+1i}^{3})=h_{i}^{3}-6e_{i+1i}e_{ii+1}h_{i}-3h_{i}^{2}+4h_{i},

we find
h_{i}^{3}\in\LM(I_{k})
for
1\leq i<k
.
This proves that
I_{k}
has finite codimension. Now, since
z_{2,k},\ldots,z_{k,k}
is a set of Casimir generators,
I_{k}\cap Z(U(L))
is a maximal ideal of
Z(U(L))
, and so Lemma
2.1.6
shows that
I_{k}
is a maximal ideal of
U(L)
, implying
I_{k}=\ker\Ad_{k}
.

(2)

The representation
\phi_{k}
is the direct sum of the adjoint representation
\Ad_{k}
and the trivial representation
\rho_{0}
, so
\ker\phi_{k}=\ker\Ad_{k}\cap\ker\rho_{0}=I_{k}\cap U^{*}(L)
, where
U^{*}(L)
is the nonunital universal enveloping algebra of
L
; i.e.,
\ker\phi_{k}
is formed by those elements of
I_{k}
which do not have a nonzero constant term.
We first change the set of Casimir generators to get rid of unnecessary constant terms in the generators of
I_{k}
. By Lemma
3.4.4
we have
\lambda_{2,k}=2k\neq 0
, hence the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\cdots&\cdots&0\\
\lambda_{3,k}&-\lambda_{2,k}&0&\cdots&0\\
\lambda_{4,k}&0&-\lambda_{2,k}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\lambda_{k,k}&0&\cdots&0&-\lambda_{2,k}\end{pmatrix}

is invertible. Therefore the central elements
c_{2,k}
and
z^{\prime}_{p,k}
for
3\leq p\leq k
form another set of Casimir generators such that
\chi_{k}(c_{2,k})=\lambda_{2,k}
,
\chi_{k}(z^{\prime}_{p,k})=0
for
3\leq p\leq k
. Then

\ker Ad_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3},z_{2k},z^{\prime}_{3,k},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{p,k}\rangle

with
e_{12}^{3},z^{\prime}_{3,k},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{p,k}\in U^{*}(\SL)
. Put
I:=\langle e_{12}^{3},z^{\prime}_{3,k},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{p,k}\rangle
,
J:=\langle z_{2,k}\rangle
and
M:=U^{*}(L)
. Then
\ker\phi_{k}=(I+J)\cap M=I+J\cap M
because
I\subseteq M
. Since
M
is a maximal ideal and
z_{2,k}\not\in M
,
J+M=U(L)
, whence

JM\subseteq J\cap M=U(L)(J\cap M)=(J+M)(J\cap M)\subseteq JM+MJ=JM

since
z_{2,k}
is central. This shows
J\cap M=JM
. Moreover, since
\SL
is simple,
U^{*}(L)=\langle e_{12}\rangle
, so
JM=\langle z_{2,k})\I(e_{12}\rangle=\langle z_{2,k}e_{12}\rangle
because
z_{2,k}
is central. Therefore

\ker\phi_{k}=I+JM=\langle e_{12}^{3},e_{12}z_{2,k},z_{3,k}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{k,k}^{\prime}\rangle.

Now repeat the argument above with
I:=\langle e_{12}^{3}\rangle
,
J:=\langle z_{2,k},\ldots,z_{k,k}\rangle
to arrive at

\ker\phi_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3},\allowbreak e_{12}z_{2,k},\ldots,e_{12}z_{k,k}\rangle
. Since
[x,yz_{p,k}]=[x,y]z_{p,k}
for all
x,y\in U(L)
and
\langle e_{1p}\rangle=U^{*}(L)
for all
2\leq p\leq k
, we get
\langle e_{12}z_{p,k}\rangle=\langle e_{1p}z_{p,k}\rangle
for all
3\leq p\leq k
, so
\ker\phi_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3},e_{12}z_{2,k},\ldots,e_{1k}z_{k,k}\rangle
.

(3)

The elements
e_{1i}
correspond to different roots
\alpha_{i}
of
\SL
and as such are weight vectors of different weights for the adjoint action of
\SL
on
U(L)
. The identity
[h_{i},e_{1p}z_{p,k}]=\alpha_{p}(h_{i})e_{1p}z_{p,k}
for
1\leq i<k
,
2\leq p\leq k
shows that the elements
e_{1p}z_{p,k}
are also weight vectors of
U(L)
of different weights, which are also different from the weight
3\alpha_{2}
of the weight vector
e_{12}^{3}
. Since ideals of
U(L)
are
L
-ideals for the adjoint action, by the primitive element lemma (Lemma
2.2.2
) we find

\ker\phi_{k}=\langle e_{12}^{3},e_{12}z_{2,k},\ldots,e_{1k}z_{k,k}\rangle=\langle e_{12}^{3}+e_{12}z_{2,k}+\cdots+e_{1k}z_{k,k}\rangle
.
∎

Now we turn back to
U(L)^{\op}
.

Corollary 3.4.6

(
Generator of
\ker\phi
)
.

\ker\phi=\langle e_{12}^{3}+e_{12}z_{2,k}+e_{13}z_{3,k}+\cdots+e_{1k}z_{k,k}\rangle.

Proof.

We have
\phi=(\phi_{k})^{\op}
, so
\ker\phi=\ker\phi_{k}
as sets with
z:=e_{12}^{3}+e_{12}z_{2,k}+e_{13}z_{3,k}+\cdots+e_{1k}z_{k,k}
generating
\ker\phi_{k}
in
U(L)
, and since
z_{p,k}\in Z(U(L))
for
2\leq p<k
,
z
generates
\ker\phi
in
U(L)^{\op}
.
∎

3.5.
U
-polynomials and
U
-identities

​​ 3.5.1
.

Modifying the first index of an exponent.

As we will see below in
4.1.2
, it is the second basis element in the subindex of the endomorphisms
\varphi_{ab}
which carries the weight of the identities of
M_{k}(F)
. The first basis element is not that relevant, and in fact it can be freely changed by substitution: On the one hand, if
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a
U
-identity of
(L,U)
-algebra
A
and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in U
, then
f(x_{1}^{u_{1}},\ldots,x_{n}^{u_{n}})
is a
U
-identity of
A
. On the other hand,

(x^{ab})^{bc}=x^{ac}
for
a,b,c\in\mathcal{S}
. Therefore, if variable
x^{ab}
with
a,b\in\mathcal{S}
features in a
U
-identity of
A
, there is an analogous
U
-identity replacing
x^{ab}
with variable
x^{cb}=(x^{ca})^{ab}
for any
c\in\mathcal{S}
. Hence we can fix one element
a\in\mathcal{S}
and assume that each endomorphism appearing as exponent in a
U
-identity is either
\varphi_{gg}
or of the form
\varphi_{ab}
for some
b\in\mathcal{S}
; that is, when looking for the generating identities of the
T_{U}
-ideal of
A
we may assume that all exponents different from
\varphi_{gg}
start with the same first basis element
a
. Taking this into account, for a fixed and previously specified
a\in\mathcal{S}
, we will write
x^{b}
as a shorthand for
x^{ab}
(
x^{\varphi_{ab}}
) with
b\in\SL
(this should not be confused with element
x^{b}\in\FL
); we will also write
x^{g}
as a shorthand for
x^{gg}
(
x^{\varphi_{gg}}
).
In particular, in this format, the bracket formula takes the simple form
(x^{b})^{C}=x^{[c,b]}
for
b,c\in\SL
and
C=\ad_{c}
.

​​ 3.5.2
.

Fixed-exponents components of
U
-identities.

We expand and add rigor to
3.5.1
. Let us write multilinear
U
-polynomials of
P_{n}^{U}
by grouping their terms with respect to the exponents of their variables, with one set of indices
\mathcal{I}
for variables of the form
x_{i}^{gg}
and another set of indices
\mathcal{J}
for variables of the form
x_{j}^{a_{j}b_{j}}
with
a_{j},b_{j}\in\mathcal{S}
, and taking into account how the first exponent indices
a_{j}
are paired with the variables
x_{j}
.
For
n\geq 1
, let
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
denote the set of pairs of sets
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
such that
\{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\}
is a partition of the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
into two disjoint subsets, one of which may be empty (observe that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
and
(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})
are different elements belonging to
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
). Then for
f\in P_{n}^{U}
we have the decomposition

f=\sum_{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{|\mathcal{J}|}}f_{\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a}},

where for fixed
\mathcal{I}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
,
\mathcal{J}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-r}\}
and
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-r})
, the
U
-polynomial
f_{\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a}}
denotes the sum of all terms of
f
in which only the variables

x_{i_{1}}^{gg},\dots,x_{i_{r}}^{gg}\text{ and }x_{j_{1}}^{a_{1}b_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{n-r}}^{a_{n-r}b_{n-r}}\text{ for any }(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-r})\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}

appear, in any order. Call
f_{\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a}}
the
(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a})

fixed-exponents component
of
f
.

Any
U
-polynomial whose only nonzero fixed-exponents component is
(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a})
is of the form

f(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a},\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\}):=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathcal{M}^{n}\\
b_{i}=g\text{ if and only if }\sigma(i)\in\mathcal{I}\end{array}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\,x_{\sigma(1)}^{a_{\sigma(1)}b_{1}}\cdots x_{\sigma(n)}^{a_{\sigma(n)}b_{n}},

where
a_{i}:=g
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
,
S_{n}
is the symmetric group acting on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\in F
. If the first exponent index is
homogeneous
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{a}=(a,\ldots,a)
for
a\in\mathcal{S}
, then we write
f(\mathcal{I},a,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})
. If
\mathcal{I}=\{1,\ldots,r\}
then we write
f(r,\boldsymbol{a},\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})
and say that
f

has first
r

g
-exponents
.
We show that to study
P_{n}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
it is enough to study the
U
-identities with only one nonzero fixed-exponents component, with homogeneous first exponent index and first
r

g
-exponents.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Let
A
be an
(L,U)
-algebra,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
with
\mathcal{I}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
and
\mathcal{J}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-r}\}
,
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{n-r}})\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}
, and
c\in\mathcal{S}
.

(1)

f\in P_{n}^{U}
is a
U
-identity of
A
if and only if any nonzero fixed-exponents component of
f
is a
U
-identity of
A
.

(2)

f(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a},\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})
is a
U
-identity of
A
if and only if
f(\mathcal{I},c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})
is a
U
-identity of
A
.

(3)

f(\mathcal{I},c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})
is a
U
-identity of
A
if and only if
f(r,c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})
is a
U
-identity of
A
.

Proof.

Along the proof we use repeatedly that if
f\in\I^{U}(A)
and
\rho
is a substitution endomorphism swapping variables then
\rho(f)\in\I^{U}(A)
(see
2.3.2
).

(1)

One implication is clear from the definition, since any multilinear
U
-polynomial is the sum of its fixed-exponents components. For the other one, fix
f\in P_{n}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
and for
j\in\mathcal{J}
let
\rho_{j}
denote the substitution endomorphism sending variable
x_{j}
to
x_{j}^{a_{j}a_{j}}
. Then, since
\varphi_{a_{j}a_{j}}\varphi_{a_{j}b}=\varphi_{a_{j}b}
and
\varphi_{a_{j}a_{j}}\varphi_{cd}=0
for
c\neq a_{j}
,

f_{\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a}}=\rho_{j_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\rho_{j_{n-r}}(f).

(2)

For
j\in\mathcal{J}
let
\rho^{c}_{j}
denote the substitution endomorphism sending variable
x_{j}
to
x_{j}^{ca_{j}}
. Then

f(\mathcal{I},c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})=\rho^{c}_{j_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\rho^{c}_{j_{n-r}}(f(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a},\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})).

Analogously, for
j\in\mathcal{J}
let
\rho^{j}_{c}
denote the substitution endomorphism sending variable
x_{j}
to
x_{j}^{a_{j}c}
. Then

f(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a},\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})=\rho^{j_{1}}_{c}\circ\cdots\circ\rho^{j_{n-r}}_{c}(f(\mathcal{I},c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})).

(3)

For
\sigma\in S_{n}
let
\rho_{\sigma}
denote the substitution endomorphism sending variable
x_{i}
to
x_{\sigma(i)}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
\tau
be the permutation sending
i_{s}
to
s
for
1\leq s\leq r
and
j_{s}
to
s+r
for
1\leq s\leq n-s
.
Then

f(r,c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})=\rho_{\tau}(f(\mathcal{I},c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\}))\text{ and }f(\mathcal{I},c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})=\rho_{\tau^{-1}}(f(r,c,\{\alpha_{\sigma,\boldsymbol{b}}\})).\qed

Moreover, it is now clear that given a set of
U
-identities, we can homogeneously fix one single element
a\in\mathcal{S}
as the first exponent index of all
U
-identities in the set, first exponent index which we may elide.

​​ 3.5.4
.

Formula for the
U
-codimensions.

For
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
and
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{|\mathcal{J}|}
, we denote by
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}
the subspace of
P_{n}^{U}
composed of the multilinear
U
-polynomials whose only nonzero fixed-exponents component is
(\mathcal{I},\boldsymbol{a})
(see
3.5.2
). For
0\leq r\leq n
and
a\in\mathcal{S}
, we denote
P^{U}_{r,n-r,a}:=P^{U}_{\{1,\ldots,r\},\{r+1,\ldots,n\},(a,\ldots,a)}
, the vector space of multilinear
U
-polynomials with only one nonzero fixed-exponents component, with homogeneous first exponent index
a
and first
r

g
-exponents. If
A
is an
(L,U)
-algebra, we denote
P_{r,n-r,a}^{U}(A):=\displaystyle\dfrac{P_{r,n-r,a}^{U}}{P_{r,n-r,a}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}

and
c^{U}_{r,n-r}(A):=\dim_{F}P^{U}_{r,n-r,a}(A)
for any
a\in\mathcal{S}
(it is independent of
a
, as deduced from item (1) of the following proposition).
We show that to study
P_{n}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
it is enough to study
P^{U}_{r,n-r,a}\cap\I^{U}(A)
for some fixed
a\in\mathcal{S}
.

Proposition 3.5.5
.

Let
A
be an
(L,U)
-algebra.

(1)

For
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
such that
r=|\mathcal{I}|
and any
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}
and
c\in\mathcal{S}
there is a linear isomorphism between
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}
and
P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}
sending
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
to
P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
.

(2)

c_{n}^{U}(A)=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}c^{U}_{r,n-r}(A).

Proof.

(1)

The proof of item (2) of Lemma
3.5.3
shows a linear isomorphism between
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}
and
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},(c,\ldots,c)}^{U}
such that
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)\cong P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},(c,\ldots,c)}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
, while the proof of item (3) shows a linear isomorphism between
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},(c,\ldots,c)}^{U}
and
P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}
such that
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},(c,\ldots,c)}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)\cong P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
(the isomorphisms being given by the invertible substitutions specified there).

(2)

By definition of fixed-exponents components we have

P_{n}^{U}=\bigoplus_{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2},\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{|\mathcal{J}|}}P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}.

This identity, combined with Lemma
3.5.3
(1), leads to

P_{n}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)=\bigoplus_{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2},\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{|\mathcal{J}|}}(P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)).

So

P_{n}^{U}(A)=\dfrac{P_{n}^{U}}{P_{n}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}\cong\bigoplus_{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2},\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{|\mathcal{J}|}}\dfrac{P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}}{P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}.

Now fix
c\in\mathcal{S}
. By item (1), for each
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
such that
|\mathcal{I}|=r
and
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{|\mathcal{J}|}
there is an isomorphism between
\dfrac{P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}}{P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}
and
\dfrac{P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}}{P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}
. Therefore,

P_{n}^{U}(A)\cong\bigoplus_{0\leq r\leq n,\,(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}\,|\,|\mathcal{I}|=r,\,\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}}\dfrac{P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}}{P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}=\bigoplus_{0\leq r\leq n}\binom{n}{r}(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}\dfrac{P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}}{P_{r,n-r,c}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)},

since for any
0\leq r\leq n
we have
\binom{n}{r}
choices of the first
r
variables and
(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}
distinct elements in
\mathcal{S}^{n-r}
.
Hence Formula (
2
) follows.∎

Fixed
a\in\mathcal{S}
, when no confusion may arise, we will write
P^{U}_{r,n-r}:=P_{r,n-r,a}^{U}
,
P^{U}_{r,n-r}(A):=P_{r,n-r,a}^{U}(A).

​​ 3.5.6
.

U
-cocharacter and its decomposition.
In view of the previous sections, instead of considering the usual permutations of variables as the actions of the symmetric groups on the spaces of multilinear polynomials, we consider the actions which permute variables together with their first index exponent, which is more natural in this context.
Denoting by
S_{\mathcal{I}}
and
S_{\mathcal{J}}
the symmetric groups acting on the sets
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
respectively, the group
S_{\mathcal{I}}\times S_{\mathcal{J}}
acts on
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}
on the left in the following way:

(\sigma,\tau)(x_{\rho(1)}^{a_{\rho(1)}b_{1}}\cdots x_{\rho(n)}^{a_{\rho(n)}b_{n}}):=x_{\pi\rho(1)}^{a_{\pi\rho(1)}b_{1}}\cdots x_{\pi\rho(n)}^{a_{\pi\rho(n)}b_{n}}

where
\rho\in S_{n}
,
a_{\rho(i)}=g=b_{i}
if
\rho(i)\in\mathcal{I}
,
(\sigma,\tau)\in S_{\mathcal{I}}\times S_{\mathcal{J}}
, and
\pi(i):=\sigma(i)
if
i\in\mathcal{I}
while
\pi(i):=\tau(i)
if
i\in\mathcal{J}
.
In this way
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}
becomes an
S_{\mathcal{I}}\times S_{\mathcal{J}}
-module.
If
A
is an
(L,U)
-algebra, then
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)
is invariant under the
S_{\mathcal{I}}\times S_{\mathcal{J}}
-action, making

P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}(A):=\frac{P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}}{P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}\cap\I^{U}(A)}

an
S_{\mathcal{I}}\times S_{\mathcal{J}}
-module with the induced action.

If
|\mathcal{I}|=r
, then
S_{\mathcal{I}}\times S_{\mathcal{J}}\cong S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
where
S_{r}
and
S_{n-r}
denote the symmetric groups acting on the sets
\{1,\dots,r\}
and
\{r+1,\dots,n\}
, respectively. Thus, for any
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
such that
|\mathcal{I}|=r
and
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}
,
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}(A)
can be regarded as an
S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
-module. As a consequence, the space

P_{(n;r)}^{U}(A):=\bigoplus_{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}\,|\,|\mathcal{I}|=r,\,\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}}P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}(A)

is also an
S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
-module, whose character we denote by
\chi_{(n;r)}^{U}(A)
and call the
(n,r)
th
U
-cocharacter of A.

Now, fixed
a\in\mathcal{S}
, the space
P^{U}_{r,n-r}(A)
(defined in
3.5.4
) is an
S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
-module whose character we denote by
\chi_{r,n-r}^{U}(A)
(it is independent of a, as deduced Proposition
3.5.5
(1)). From the same result we know that for each
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{[n]}{2}
such that
|\mathcal{I}|=r
and
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-r}
there is an isomorphism of vector spaces between
P_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\boldsymbol{a}}^{U}(A)
and
P^{U}_{r,n-r}(A)
, and since the
S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
-action commutes with the isomorphism, it is in addition an isomorphism of
S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
-modules. Therefore

P_{(n;r)}^{U}(A)\underset{S_{r}\times S_{n-r}}{\cong\phantom{m}}\binom{n}{r}(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}P^{U}_{r,n-r}(A).

(3.1)

Recall that the irreducible
S_{r}\times S_{n-r}
-characters are the tensor products
\chi_{\lambda}\otimes\chi_{\mu}
of the irreducible
S_{r}
- and
S_{n-r}
-characters
\chi_{\lambda}
and
\chi_{\mu}
, where
\lambda\vdash r
and
\mu\vdash n-r
are partitions.
Since
\chr(F)=0
, by complete reducibility we can write

\chi_{r,n-r}^{U}(A)=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash(r,n-r)}m_{\lambda,\mu}\,\chi_{\lambda}\otimes\chi_{\mu},

(3.2)

where
\lambda\vdash r
,
\mu\vdash n-r
, and
m_{\lambda,\mu}\geq 0
is the multiplicity corresponding to
\chi_{\lambda}\otimes\chi_{\mu}
. Thus, as a consequence of (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), the
(n,r)
th
U
-cocharacter of A can be decomposed as

\chi_{(n;r)}^{U}(A)=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash(r,n-r)}\binom{n}{r}(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}m_{\lambda,\mu}\,\chi_{\lambda}\otimes\chi_{\mu}.

4.
Differential identities of
M_{k}(F)

In this section we determine the
U
-identities,
(L,U)
-identities, and
L
-identities of the algebra
M_{k}(F)
for
k\geq 2
.

4.1.
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)

​​ 4.1.1
.

Multiplication table of
M_{k}(F)
.

The multiplication table arising from
\mathcal{M}
(see
3.2.1
), with results expressed in matrix units, is summarized by the following relations:

(1)

gx=x=xg
for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
.

(2)

e_{ij}e_{jk}=e_{ik}
,
e_{ij}e_{kl}=0
(
j\neq k
).

(3)

h_{i}e_{ij}=e_{ij}
,
h_{i}e_{i+1,j}=-e_{i+1,j}
,
e_{ij}h_{j}=e_{ij}
,
e_{i,j+1}h_{j}=-e_{i,j+1}
,
h_{i}e_{jk}=0
(
j\not\in\{i,i+1\}
),
e_{ij}h_{k}=0
(
k\not\in\{j,j+1\}
).

(4)

h_{i}^{2}=e_{ii}+e_{i+1,i+1}
,
h_{i}h_{i+1}=-e_{i+1,i+1}=h_{i+1}h_{i}
,
h_{i}h_{j}=0
(
j\not\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}
).

​​ 4.1.2
.

Generating
U
-identities.

Due to the nature of the endomorphisms
\varphi_{ab}
(see Formula (
3.2.2
)), the identities in the multiplication table of
M_{k}(F)
(see
4.1.1
) translate well to
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)
: for example, if
x,y\in M_{k}(F)
and
a,b\in\mathcal{S}
then

x^{ae_{ij}}y^{be_{lm}}=\mu_{a}^{x}\mu_{b}^{y}e_{ij}e_{lm}=0\text{ if }j\neq l.

This idea provides us at once with the following
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)
in two variables.

Lemma 4.1.3

(
U
-identities from multiplication table
)
.

Fix
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
. For
1\leq i\leq r
, fix
\sigma_{i}\in S_{2}
and let
\alpha_{i}\in F
and
m^{i}_{1},m^{i}_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
be such that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}m^{i}_{1}m^{i}_{2}=0
in
M_{k}(F)
, with
a_{1}=g
(resp.
a_{2}=g
) forcing
m^{i}_{\sigma_{i}(1)}=g
(resp.
m^{i}_{\sigma_{i}(2)}=g
) for all
1\leq i\leq r
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}x^{a_{\sigma_{i}(1)}m^{i}_{1}}_{\sigma_{i}(1)}x^{a_{\sigma_{i}(2)}m^{i}_{2}}_{\sigma_{i}(2)}\in\I^{U}(M_{k}(F)).

Proof.

Evaluating in
x_{1},x_{2}\in M_{k}(F)
, by definition of
\varphi_{ab}
we get

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}x^{a_{\sigma_{i}(1)}m^{i}_{1}}_{\sigma_{i}(1)}x^{a_{\sigma_{i}(2)}m^{i}_{2}}_{\sigma_{i}(2)}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu^{x_{\sigma_{i}(1)}}_{a_{\sigma_{i}(1)}}\mu^{x_{\sigma_{i}(2)}}_{a_{\sigma_{i}(2)}}\alpha_{i}m^{i}_{1}m^{i}_{2}=\mu^{x_{1}}_{a_{1}}\mu^{x_{2}}_{a_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}m^{i}_{1}m^{i}_{2}=0.\qed

We will show in the following that all
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)
can be generated from
U
-identities in two variables, with at most two terms, arising from its multiplication table as in Lemma
4.1.3
.

Recall that notation
x^{b}
with
x\in\FU
,
b\in\mathcal{S}
is shorthand for the element
x^{\varphi_{ab}}\in\FU
, for a fixed and elided first exponent index
a\in\mathcal{S}
which we will not explicitly mention in the next results (see
3.5.1
). Similarly we write
P^{U}_{r,n-r}
instead of
P^{U}_{r,n-r,a}
(see
3.5.4
).

We start the description of the
U
-identities and codimensions of
M_{k}(F)
by appealing to the linear structure of the
T_{U}
-ideal. We will tackle the simpler case
k=2
separately.

Proposition 4.1.4
.

The
T_{U}
-ideal of
U
-identities of
M_{2}(F)
is generated by the following
U
-polynomials:

[x^{g},y^{g}],\ [x^{g},y^{a}],\ x^{a}y^{b}-y^{a}x^{b},\ x^{c}y^{c},\ x^{h_{1}}y^{c}+x^{c}y^{h_{1}},\ x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{21}}+x^{e_{21}}y^{e_{12}}-x^{h_{1}}y^{h_{1}},

where
a,b\in\{h_{1},e_{12},e_{21}\}
and
c\in\{e_{12},e_{21}\}
. In addition,
c_{n}^{U}(M_{2}(F))=4^{n+1}-3(n+1).

Proof.

Firstly, note that
M_{2}(F)
has no nontrivial
U
-identities of degree
1
(as
x^{\varphi}=0
implies
\varphi=0
for any
\varphi\in\E(M_{2}(F))
), and so
c_{1}^{U}(M_{2}(F))=\dim_{F}(P_{1}^{U})=\dim_{F}(U)=3^{2}+1=4^{2}-3\cdot 2
as needed. Hence in the following, we assume
n\geq 2
.

Let
I
be the
T_{U}
-ideal generated by the
U
-polynomials in the statement of the proposition. We will show that
I=\I^{U}(M_{2}(F))
.
Recall that
\mathcal{S}=\{h_{1},e_{12},e_{21}\}
for
k=2
by definition. By Lemma
4.1.3
it follows that
I\subseteq\I^{U}(M_{2}(F))
.

In order to prove the opposite inclusion let
f\in\I^{U}(M_{2}(F))
with
\deg f=n
and assume, as we may, that
f
is multilinear and
f\in P^{U}_{r,n-r}
, where
0\leq r\leq n
(see
3.5.2
and
3.5.4
). We will prove that
f\underset{I}{\equiv}0
.

For all
1\leq i\leq n-r
, in order to simplify the notation, let us rename
x_{r+i}^{a}
to
y_{i}^{a}
,
a\in\mathcal{S}
, so that variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
correspond to exponents
g
and variables
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-r}
correspond to exponents
a\in\mathcal{S}
. Since
[x_{1}^{g},x_{2}^{g}],\ [x^{g},y^{a}]\in I
for all
a\in\mathcal{S}
,
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials of type

x_{1}^{g}\cdots x_{r}^{g}y_{i_{1}}^{a_{1}}\cdots y_{i_{n-r}}^{a_{n-r}}

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{n-r}\in\mathcal{S}
. If
r=n
we have
f\underset{I}{\equiv}\alpha x_{1}^{g}\cdots x^{g}_{n}
for some
\alpha\in F
; by evaluating
x_{i}=g
for
1\leq i\leq n
we find, since
f
is a
U
-identity of
M_{2}(F)
,

0=f(g,\ldots,g)\underset{I}{\equiv}\alpha(g)^{gg}\cdots(g)^{gg}=\alpha g^{n}=\alpha g,

hence
\alpha=0
and
f\underset{I}{\equiv}0
.
If
r=n-1
then we have

f\underset{I}{\equiv}\alpha_{1}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{h_{1}}+\alpha_{2}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{e_{12}}+\alpha_{3}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{e_{21}}

for some
\alpha_{i}\in F
,
1\leq i\leq 3
; by evaluating
x_{i}=g
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
y_{1}=h_{1}+e_{12}+e_{21}
we analogously get,
for some
a\in\{h_{1},e_{12},e_{21}\}
,

\alpha_{1}(g)^{gg}\cdots(g)^{gg}(h_{1}+e_{12}+e_{21})^{ah_{1}}+\cdots+\alpha_{3}(g)^{gg}\cdots(g)^{gg}(h_{1}+e_{12}+e_{21})^{ae_{21}}=\alpha_{1}h_{1}+\alpha_{2}e_{12}+\alpha_{3}e_{21}=0

and hence
\alpha_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq 3
, and
f\underset{I}{\equiv}0
.

Now let us assume that
0\leq r\leq n-2
. Since
y_{1}^{a}y_{2}^{b}-y_{2}^{a}y_{1}^{b}\in I
with
a,b\in\mathcal{S}
, it is possible to reorder the variables
y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}
in each
U
-monomial of
f
modulo
I
without reordering their original exponents. Moreover, since
y_{1}^{c}y_{2}^{c}\in I
for
c\in\{e_{12},e_{21}\}
, modulo
I
the nonzero terms of
f
do not have two variables with exponent
e_{12}
nor with exponent
e_{21}
adjacent to each other. Since in addition
y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{c}+y_{1}^{c}y_{2}^{h_{1}}\in I
for
c\in\{e_{12},e_{21}\}
, we can permute the
h_{1}
exponents with the
e_{12},e_{21}
exponents; it then follows that
f
modulo
I
can be written as a linear combination of
U
-monomials of the following forms:

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{m}^{h_{1}}y_{m+1}^{e_{12}}y_{m+2}^{e_{21}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{12}}y_{n-r}^{e_{21}},

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{m}^{h_{1}}y_{m+1}^{e_{21}}y_{m+2}^{e_{12}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{21}}y_{n-r}^{e_{12}},

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{m}^{h_{1}}y_{m+1}^{e_{12}}y_{m+2}^{e_{21}}y_{m+3}^{e_{12}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{21}}y_{n-r}^{e_{12}},

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{m}^{h_{1}}y_{m+1}^{e_{21}}y_{m+2}^{e_{12}}y_{m+3}^{e_{21}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{12}}y_{n-r}^{e_{21}},

where
0\leq m\leq n-r
. Since
y_{1}^{e_{12}}y_{2}^{e_{21}}+y_{1}^{e_{21}}y_{2}^{e_{12}}-y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{h_{1}}\in I
it follows that, if
n-r\geq 2
, we can write
f
modulo
I
as a linear combination of
U
-monomials in which at most one exponent
h_{1}
appears. Thus, if
n-r
is even, we get that

\displaystyle f\underset{I}{\equiv}

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{e_{12}}y_{3}^{e_{21}}y_{4}^{e_{12}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{21}}y_{n-r}^{e_{12}}+\alpha_{2}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{e_{21}}y_{3}^{e_{12}}y_{4}^{e_{21}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{12}}y_{n-r}^{e_{21}}+

(4.1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{e_{12}}y_{2}^{e_{21}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{12}}y_{n-r}^{e_{21}}+\alpha_{4}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{e_{21}}y_{2}^{e_{12}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{21}}y_{n-r}^{e_{12}}

for some
\alpha_{i}\in F
,
1\leq i\leq 4
, whereas if
n-r
is odd, then we have that

\displaystyle f\underset{I}{\equiv}

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{e_{12}}y_{3}^{e_{21}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{12}}y_{n-r}^{e_{21}}+\alpha_{2}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{1}^{e_{21}}y_{2}^{e_{12}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{21}}y_{n-r}^{e_{12}}+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{e_{12}}y_{2}^{e_{21}}y_{3}^{e_{12}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{21}}y_{n-r}^{e_{12}}+\alpha_{4}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{e_{21}}y_{2}^{e_{12}}y_{3}^{e_{21}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{e_{12}}y_{n-r}^{e_{21}}

for some
\alpha_{i}\in F
,
1\leq i\leq 4
.
Suppose that
f
is as in (
4.1
). By making the evaluation
x_{i}=g
for
1\leq i\leq r
and
y_{j}=h_{1}+e_{12}+e_{21}
for
1\leq j\leq n-r
we get
\alpha_{1}e_{12}-\alpha_{2}e_{21}+\alpha_{3}e_{11}+\alpha_{4}e_{22}=0
. Thus
\alpha_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq 4
, and
f
is the zero
U
-polynomial modulo
I
. One can deal similarly with the other case. Thus
\I^{U}(M_{2}(F))=I
.

The argument above also proves that

c^{U}_{r,n-r}(M_{2}(F))=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{ if }r=n,\\
3,&\mbox{ if }r=n-1,\\
4,&\mbox{ if }0\leq r\leq n-2.\end{cases}

Therefore, by Formula (
2
),

c_{n}^{U}(M_{2}(F))=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}3^{n-r}c^{U}_{r,n-r}(M_{2}(F))=4\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}3^{n-r}-3n-3=4^{n+1}-3(n+1).\qed

Next lemma follows from simple computations.

Lemma 4.1.5
.

If
k\geq 3
, then:

(1)

x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}\equiv-x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ij}}\ (\md\langle x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j-1}}-x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}},\ x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j-1}}\rangle_{T_{U}})
, where
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
2\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
,
1\leq l\leq k
;

(2)

x^{e_{1j}}y^{e_{j1}}\equiv x^{h_{1}}y^{h_{1}}+x^{h_{1}}y^{h_{2}}\ (\md\langle x^{e_{1j}}y^{e_{j1}}+x^{e_{2,l}}x_{2}^{e_{l,2}}-x^{h_{1}}x_{2}^{h_{1}},\ x^{h_{1}}x_{2}^{h_{2}}+x^{e_{2j}}x_{2}^{e_{j2}}\rangle_{T_{U}})
, where
2\leq j\leq k
,
1\leq l\leq k
,
l\neq 2
;

(3)

x^{e_{kj}}y^{e_{jk}}\equiv x^{h_{k-1}}y^{h_{k-1}}+x^{h_{k-2}}y^{h_{k-1}}\ (\md\langle x^{e_{k-1,j}}y^{e_{j,k-1}}+x^{e_{k,l}}y^{e_{l,k}}-x^{h_{k-1}}y^{h_{k-1}},\ x^{h_{k-2}}y^{h_{k-1}}+x^{e_{k-1,l}}y^{e_{l,k-1}}\rangle_{T_{U}})
, where
1\leq j\leq k-1
,
1\leq l\leq k
,
l\neq k-1
.

Proposition 4.1.6
.

The
T_{U}
-ideal of
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)
,
k\geq 3
, is generated by the following
U
-polynomials:

(1)

[x^{g},y^{g}],\ \ [x^{g},y^{a}],\ \ x^{a}y^{b}-y^{a}x^{b}
, where
a,b\in\mathcal{S}
;

(2)

x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{lm}}
, where
1\leq i,j,l,m\leq k
,
j\neq l
;

(3)

x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{jl}}
, where
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq i,i+1
,
l\neq j
;

(4)

x^{e_{jl}}y^{h_{i}}
where
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
l\neq i,i+1
,
j\neq l
;

(5)

x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j-1}}
, where
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
2\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
;

(6)

x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j}}
, where
2\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq j\leq k-1
,
j\neq i
;

(7)

x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}
, where
2\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq i
,
i,j\neq l
;

(8)

x^{h_{i-1}}y^{h_{i}}+x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{ji}}
, where
2\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
;

(9)

x^{h_{i}}y^{h_{j}}
, where
1\leq i,j\leq k-1
,
j\neq i-1,i,i+1
, if
k\geq 4
;

(10)

[x^{h_{i}},y^{h_{i+1}}]
, where
1\leq i\leq k-2
;

(11)

x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{ji}}+x^{e_{i+1l}}y^{e_{li+1}}-x^{h_{i}}y^{h_{i}}
, where
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq i
,
l\neq i+1
;

(12)

x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j-1}}+x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}
, where
1\leq i,l\leq k
,
2\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
,
i,j\neq l
.

In addition,
c_{n}^{U}(M_{k}(F))=k^{2(n+1)}-(k^{2}-1)(n+1).

Proof.

The proof of this result follows a scheme similar to that of Proposition
4.1.4
for
2\times 2
matrices, with different computations. Firstly, note that there are no nontrivial identities of degree
1
and thus
c_{1}^{U}(M_{k}(F))=\dim_{F}(U)=(k^{2}-1)^{2}+1=k^{4}-2(k^{2}-1)
as expected, so henceforth we assume
n\geq 2
. Let
I
be the
T_{U}
-ideal generated by the
U
-polynomials in the statement of the proposition. By Lemma
4.1.3
it follows that
I\subseteq\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
. To prove the opposite inclusion, first, we find a set of generators of
P^{U}_{r,n-r}
modulo
P^{U}_{r,n-r}\cap I
, for each
n\geq 1
and
0\leq r\leq n
, and after that, we show, by evaluation, that the sets of generators found are actually bases of their corresponding vector spaces.

Let
f\in P^{U}_{r,n-r}
be a multilinear
U
-polynomial of degree
n
. In order to simplify the notation, for all
1\leq i\leq n-r
let us rename
x_{r+i}^{a}
to
y_{i}^{a}
,
a\in\mathcal{S}
, so that variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
correspond to exponents
g
and variables
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-r}
correspond to exponents
a\in\mathcal{S}
. Since
[x^{g}_{1},x^{g}_{2}],\ [x^{g},y^{a}]\in I
for all
a\in\mathcal{S}
,
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials of type

x_{1}^{g}\cdots x_{r}^{g}y_{i_{1}}^{a_{1}}\cdots y_{i_{n-r}}^{a_{n-r}}

where
a_{i_{1}},\dots,a_{i_{n-r}}\in\mathcal{S}
. So
P^{U}_{n,0}
is generated modulo
P^{U}_{n,0}\cap I
by the
U
-monomial
x_{1}^{g}\cdots x_{n}^{g}
. If
r=n-1
then
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials

x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k-1\text{ and }x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{e_{jl}}\text{ for }1\leq j,l\leq k,j\neq l.

(4.2)

It follows that
P^{U}_{n-1,1}
is generated modulo
P^{U}_{n-1,1}\cap I
by the
U
-monomials in (
4.2
).

Now suppose that
0\leq r\leq n-2
. By
U
-identities
3
-
6
, in each
U
-monomial of
f
we can move to the left, modulo
I
, all the variables with exponent
h_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
; moreover, since
y_{1}^{a}y_{2}^{b}-y_{2}^{a}y_{1}^{b}\in I
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{S}
, we can always reorder the indices of the variables with exponent in
\mathcal{S}
. Call (P1) to this moving and reordering procedure. From (P1) it follows that
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials of type

x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i_{1}}}\cdots y_{s}^{h_{i_{s}}}y_{s+1}^{b_{1}}\cdots y_{n-r}^{b_{n-r}}

for
0\leq s\leq n-r
,
1\leq i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\leq k-1
and
b_{1},\dots,b_{n-r}\in\{e_{ij}\ |\ 1\leq i,j\leq k,i\neq j\}
.
Since
y_{1}^{e_{ij}}y_{2}^{e_{lm}}\in I
for
1\leq i,j,l,m\leq k
,
j\neq l
, we can require in addition that the sequence of exponents
b_{1},\dots,b_{n-r}
has nonzero product
b_{1}\cdots b_{n-r}
, i.e., if
b_{t}=e_{ij}
and
b_{t+1}=e_{lm}
then
j=l
. Notice that the
U
-identities of Lemma
4.1.5
also belong to
I
, thus by
U
-identities
7
,
8
and Lemma
4.1.5
we can reduce each pair
y_{t}^{e_{il}}y_{t+1}^{e_{lj}}
:

•

If
i\neq j,k
, to the pair
y_{t}^{h_{i}}y_{t+1}^{e_{ij}}
.

•

If
i=k\neq j
, to the pair
y_{t}^{h_{k-1}}y_{t+1}^{e_{kj}}
.

•

if
i=j
, to a linear combination of one or two
U
-monomials of the form
y_{t}^{h_{l}}y_{t+1}^{h_{m}}
with
1\leq l\leq k-1
and
m\in\{l,l+1\}
.

To this reduction procedure call (P2). By applying (P1) and (P2) repeatedly, we find that
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials of types

x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i_{1}}}\cdots y_{n-r}^{h_{i_{n-r}}}\text{ and }x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i_{1}}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{i_{n-r-1}}}y_{n-r}^{e_{ij}}

for
1\leq i_{1},\dots,i_{n-r}\leq k-1
and
1\leq i,j\leq k
,
i\neq j
. By
U
-identity
9
we can assume that the product of the exponents
h_{i_{1}}\cdots h_{i_{n-r}}
is nonzero, and by
U
-identity
10
we can assume that
i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{n-r}
; hence we may assume that
i_{j+1}\in\{i_{j},i_{j}+1\}
for all
0\leq j<n-r
.
Moreover, since for
k\geq 4
, by
U
-identities
9
,
10
we have

y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{h_{i+1}}y_{3}^{h_{i+2}}=[y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{h_{i+1}}]y_{3}^{h_{i+2}}+y_{2}^{h_{i+1}}(y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{3}^{h_{i+2}})\in I

for
1\leq i\leq k-3
, we can assume that, for all
k\geq 3
, in each
U
-monomial of
f
modulo
I
there are at most two distinct (and consecutive) exponents
h_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq k-1
), the rest of them being copies of one of those.
In addition, by
U
-identities
8
(applied twice) and
11
we find

\displaystyle y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{h_{i+1}}+y_{1}^{h_{i-1}}y_{2}^{h_{i}}+y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{h_{i}}=

\displaystyle(y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{h_{i+1}}+y_{1}^{e_{i+1j}}y_{2}^{e_{ji+1}})+(y_{1}^{h_{i-1}}y_{2}^{h_{i}}+y_{1}^{e_{ij}}y_{2}^{e_{ji}})-(y_{1}^{e_{ij}}y_{2}^{e_{ji}}+y_{1}^{e_{i+1j}}y_{2}^{e_{ji+1}}-y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{h_{i}})\in I

for all
2\leq i\leq k-2
, so by recursion on
i
we may suppose that, in each
U
-monomial of
f
, either all exponents
h_{i}
are equal (for some
1\leq i\leq k-1
) or there are two distinct exponents,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
. Now notice that by
U
-identities
11
,
8
,
3
we have

y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{h_{2}}y_{3}^{h_{2}}+y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{h_{1}}y_{3}^{h_{2}}=y_{1}^{h_{1}}(y_{2}^{h_{2}}y_{3}^{h_{2}}-y_{2}^{e_{21}}y_{3}^{e_{12}}-y_{2}^{e_{31}}y_{3}^{e_{13}})+y_{1}^{h_{1}}(y_{2}^{h_{1}}y_{3}^{h_{2}}+y_{2}^{e_{21}}y_{3}^{e_{12}})+(y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{e_{31}})y_{3}^{e_{13}}\in I,

so
y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{h_{2}}y_{3}^{h_{2}}\underset{I}{\equiv}-y_{1}^{h_{1}}y_{2}^{h_{1}}y_{3}^{h_{2}}
. Hence
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials of types

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\cdots y_{n-r}^{h_{i}},\quad

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{1}}y_{n-r}^{h_{2}},

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{i}}y_{n-r}^{e_{lj}},\quad

\displaystyle x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{n-r-2}^{h_{1}}y_{n-r-1}^{h_{2}}y_{n-r}^{e_{lj}},

for
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
l\neq j
. Finally, since
y_{1}^{h_{i-1}}y_{2}^{e_{ij}}+y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{e_{ij}}\in\langle y_{1}^{h_{i-1}}y_{2}^{e_{ij}}+y_{1}^{e_{il}}y_{2}^{e_{lj}},\ y_{1}^{e_{ij}}y_{2}^{h_{j-1}}+y_{1}^{e_{il}}y_{2}^{e_{lj}},\ y_{1}^{h_{i}}y_{2}^{e_{ij}}+y_{1}^{e_{ij}}y_{2}^{h_{j-1}}\rangle_{T_{U}},
where
2\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq i
,
l\neq i,j
, and by
U
-identity
3
, it follows that
f
modulo
I
is a linear combination of the following
U
-monomials:

\begin{split}&x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\cdots y_{n-r}^{h_{i}},\ \ x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{1}}y_{n-r}^{h_{2}},\\
&x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{i}}y_{n-r}^{e_{ij}},\ \ x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{k-1}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{k-1}}y_{n-r}^{e_{kl}},\end{split}

(4.3)

where
1\leq i,l\leq k-1
,
1\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
. Thus we have that
P^{U}_{r,n-r}
modulo
P^{U}_{r,n-r}\cap I
is generated by the
U
-monomials in (
4.3
).

The
U
-monomial
x_{1}^{g}\cdots x_{n}^{g}
can be seen to be nonzero modulo
I
by evaluating
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{n}=g
. We next show that the
U
-monomials in (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) are linearly independent modulo
\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
if
r=n-1
or
0\leq r\leq n-2
, respectively. To that end, let us assume first that
f\in\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
is a linear combination of
U
-monomials in (
4.2
), i.e.,

f=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\alpha_{i}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{h_{i}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j,l\leq k\\
j\neq l\end{subarray}}\beta_{jl}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{n-1}y_{1}^{e_{jl}}

for some
\alpha_{i},\beta_{jl}\in F
. From the evaluation

x_{1}=\cdots=x_{n-1}=g\text{ and }y_{1}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{S}}a

we get

\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\alpha_{i}h_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j,l\leq k\\
j\neq l\end{subarray}}\beta_{jl}e_{jl}=0,

from which it follows, since
\mathcal{S}
is a linearly independent set, that
\alpha_{i}=\beta_{lj}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq l
. Therefore the
U
-monomials in (
4.2
) are linearly independent modulo
\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
.

Let us assume now that
f\in\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
is such that

\displaystyle f=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\alpha_{i}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\cdots y_{n-r}^{h_{i}}+\beta x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{1}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{1}}y_{n-r}^{h_{2}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k-1\\
1\leq j\leq k\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\gamma_{ij}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{i}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{i}}y_{n-r}^{e_{ij}}+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq l\leq k-1}\gamma_{kl}x^{g}_{1}\cdots x^{g}_{r}y_{1}^{h_{k-1}}\cdots y_{n-r-1}^{h_{k-1}}y_{n-r}^{e_{kl}}.

If we evaluate
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{r}=g
and
y_{1}=\cdots=y_{n-r}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{S}}a
, we get

\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\alpha_{i}(e_{ii}\pm e_{i+1,i+1})+\beta e_{22}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k-1\\
1\leq j\leq k\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\gamma_{ij}e_{ij}+\sum_{1\leq l\leq k-1}(-1)^{n-r-1}\gamma_{kl}e_{kl}=0,

which produces
\alpha_{i}=\beta=\gamma_{lj}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq l,j\leq k
. Therefore the elements in (
4.3
) are linearly independent modulo
P^{U}_{r,n-r}\cap\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
.

The fact that
P^{U}_{r,n-r}\cap\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))\supseteq P^{U}_{r,n-r}\cap I
for all
n\in\N
and
0\leq r\leq n
proves that
\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))=I
, with
x_{1}^{g}\cdots x_{n}^{g}
and the elements in (
4.2
), (
4.3
)
forming a basis of
P^{U}_{r,n-r}
modulo
P^{U}_{r,n-r}\cap\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
for
r=n
,
r=n-1
and
0\leq r\leq n-2
, respectively.
Thus, by counting we get

c^{U}_{r,n-r}(M_{k}(F))=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{ if }r=n,\\
k^{2}-1,&\mbox{ if }r=n-1,\\
k^{2},&\mbox{ if }0\leq r\leq n-2.\end{cases}

Hence, by Formula (
2
) it follows that

\displaystyle c_{n}^{U}(M_{k}(F))

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}c^{U}_{r,n-r}(M_{k}(F))=k^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}(k^{2}-1)^{n-r}-(k^{2}-1)n-(k^{2}-1)=

\displaystyle=k^{2(n+1)}-(k^{2}-1)(n+1).\qed

​​ 4.1.7
.

Modifying the second index of an exponent.

Through the
L
-action, the second basis element of the subindex of
\varphi_{ab}
can also be changed in the search for identities, albeit the result is less straightforward. In particular, if
d\in L
is a derivation then
(x^{ca}y^{cb})^{d}=x^{(ca)d}y^{cb}+x^{ca}y^{(cb)d}
. We will resort to this method to reduce the number of generators in the basis of
\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
.

We are working in
\FU
, which has no
U
-action (see Remarks
2.3.3
). Nevertheless, to simplify the notation, instead of writing exponents belonging to
U(L)^{\op}
whose action would eventually project to
U^{\op}
once they landed on isolated variables, we will write the exponents directly in
U^{\op}
with the caution of evaluating them only on isolated variables. For example, to apply the action of
e_{12}^{2}\in U(L)^{\op}
to
x^{u}y^{v}\in\FU
, we write

(x^{u}y^{v})^{E_{12}^{2}}=x^{uE_{12}^{2}}y^{v}+2x^{uE_{12}}y^{vE_{12}}+x^{u}y^{vE_{12}^{2}}

with
E_{12}\in U^{\op}
, and only then simplify the exponents
uE_{12}^{2},uE_{12},vE_{12},vE_{12}^{2}
by computing in
U^{\op}
.

Theorem 4.1.8
.

The
T_{U}
-ideal of
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)
is generated by the following
U
-polynomials:

(1)

Either
x^{e_{12}e_{12}}y^{e_{12}e_{12}}
if
k=2
or
x^{e_{12}e_{12}}y^{e_{12}e_{31}}
if
k\geq 3
,

(2)

x^{e_{12}e_{12}}y^{e_{12}e_{21}}-y^{e_{12}e_{12}}x^{e_{12}e_{21}}
,

(3)

[x^{gg},y^{gg}]
,

(4)

[x^{gg},y^{e_{12}e_{12}}]
.

Proof.

By Propositions
4.1.4
and
4.1.6
, the
T_{U}
-ideal of
U
-identities of
M_{k}(F)
is generated by the following list (L) of identities, for fixed
a\in\mathcal{S}
:

(1)

x^{ae_{ij}}y^{ae_{lm}}
with
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
2\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
.

(2)

x^{ah_{i}}y^{ae_{jl}}
with
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq i,i+1
,
l\neq j
;

(3)

x^{ae_{jl}}y^{ah_{i}}
with
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
l\neq i,i+1
,
j\neq l
;

(4)

x^{ah_{i}}y^{ah_{j}}
with
1\leq i,j\leq k-1
,
j\neq i-1,i,i+1
.

(5)

x^{ae_{ij}}y^{ah_{j-1}}+x^{ae_{il}}y^{ae_{lj}}
with
1\leq i,l\leq k
,
2\leq j\leq k
,
l,j\neq i
,
j\neq l
;

(6)

x^{ah_{i-1}}y^{ae_{ij}}+x^{ae_{il}}y^{ae_{lj}}
with
2\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
l,j\neq i
,
j\neq l
;

(7)

x^{ah_{i}}y^{ae_{ij}}+x^{ae_{ij}}y^{ah_{j-1}}
with
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
2\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
;

(8)

x^{ah_{i-1}}y^{ae_{ij}}+x^{ae_{ij}}y^{ah_{j}}
with
2\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq j\leq k-1
,
j\neq i
;

(9)

x^{ah_{i-1}}y^{ah_{i}}+x^{ae_{ij}}y^{ae_{ji}}
with
2\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j\leq k
,
j\neq i
;

(10)

x^{ae_{ij}}y^{ae_{ji}}+x^{ae_{i+1,l}}y^{ae_{l,i+1}}-x^{ah_{i}}y^{ah_{i}}
with
1\leq i\leq k-1
,
1\leq j,l\leq k
,
j\neq i
,
l\neq i+1
;

(11)

x^{ab}y^{ac}-y^{ab}x^{ac}
with
b,c\in\mathcal{S}
;

(12)

[x^{gg},y^{gg}],\,[x^{gg}y^{ab}]
with
b\in\mathcal{S}
;

(13)

[x^{ah_{i}},y^{ah_{i+1}}]
with
1\leq i\leq k-2
;

where some identities are not realized for
k=2
and
k=3
. Let
J
be the
T_{U}
-ideal of
\FU
generated by either
x^{e_{12}e_{12}}y^{e_{12}e_{12}}
if
k=2
or
x^{e_{12}e_{12}}y^{e_{12}e_{31}}
if
k\geq 3
,
x^{e_{12}e_{12}}y^{e_{12}e_{21}}-y^{e_{12}e_{12}}x^{e_{12}e_{21}}
,
[x^{gg},y^{gg}]
and

[x^{gg},y^{e_{12}e_{12}}]
. Clearly
J\subseteq\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
. In the following, we will prove that
J=\I^{U}(M_{k}(F))
by showing that all identities in the list (L) belong to
J
, mainly by the action of inner derivations. From now on we change, as we may, the first exponent index
a
of any variable
x^{ab}
with
b\in\mathcal{S}
to
e_{12}
and elide it by writing
x^{b}
; we also write
x^{g}
for
x^{gg}
.

By the bracket formula (
3.3.2
), the action of the inner derivation
E_{ij}
(generated by
e_{ij}
) on
x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}
gives

(x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}})^{E_{ij}}=\delta_{ja}x^{e_{ib}}y^{e_{cd}}-\delta_{bi}x^{e_{aj}}y^{e_{cd}}+\delta_{jc}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{id}}-\delta_{di}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cj}}

for
i,j,a,b,c,d\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
i\neq j,a\neq b,c\neq d
, where
\delta_{rs}
denotes Kronecker’s delta (see
3.3.2
to review the key computational facts about inner derivations that we will need in the sequel). From now on we assume without further notice that any element of the form
e_{ab}
or
E_{ab}
satisfies
a\neq b
and imposes this restriction wherever it appears.

For
U
-polynomials
f,g\in\FU
and
u\in U(L)^{\op}
let us write
f\overset{u}{\longrightarrow}g
to denote
g=f^{u}
; we call this kind of operation a
deduction
, with
f
its
starting

U
-polynomial and
g
its
ending

U
-polynomial, which is
deduced from

f
. More in general, we extend the notation and write
f\xrightarrow{u(*)}g
if some subtractions of other
U
-polynomials also deduced from
f
are needed in the process of getting
g
from
f
, in addition to the action of
u
. Notice that if the starting
U
-polynomial of a deduction belongs to a
U
-ideal
I
of
\FU
, then the ending
U
ñpolynomial of the deduction also belongs to
I
, as
I
is
U(L)^{\op}
-invariant. In the following, we will use this fact to show, in sequential steps, that the list of identities (L) belongs to
J
. When in this process we deduce that some
U
-polynomial
g
is in
J
because
f\in J
, we say that we
reach

g

starting from
f
.

(D1)

Identities with one
U
-monomial, with exponents of type
e
and 2 distinct subindices:

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{2}E_{ca}^{2}}x^{e_{cb}}y^{e_{cb}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(c\neq a)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{2}E_{bc}^{2}}x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{ac}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(c\neq b)$}}

(4.4)

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{24}E_{ba}^{4}}x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{ba}}

To aid comprehension of the rest of the computations, let us show the details of the second and third deductions. Recall that the appearance of
e_{ac}
as the ending
U
-polynomial of the second deduction implies the additional restriction
c\neq a
:

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{E_{bc}(c\neq a)}-x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{ab}}-x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ac}}\xrightarrow{E_{bc}(c\neq a)}x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{ac}}+x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{ac}}=2x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{ac}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(c\neq b)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{E_{ba}^{4}}

\displaystyle(x^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{4}}y^{e_{ab}}+4(x^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{3}}(y^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}}+6(x^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{2}}(y^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{2}}+4(x^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}}(y^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{3}}+(x^{e_{ab}})(y^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{4}}=

\displaystyle=6(x^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{2}}(y^{e_{ab}})^{E_{ba}^{2}}=24(x^{e_{ab}})^{\varphi_{e_{ab}e_{ba}}}(y^{e_{ab}})^{\varphi_{e_{ab}e_{ba}}}=24x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{ba}}

since
E_{ba}^{3}=0
and
E_{ba}^{2}=-2\varphi_{e_{ab}e_{ba}}
.

From (
(D1)
) we deduce that, starting from
x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{12}}\in J
, we can reach any
U
-monomial identity of the form
x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}
with
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, for any
k\geq 2
:

•

If
a,b\neq 1,2
, apply the first and second deductions to reach the target
U
-monomial.

•

If
a=1,b\neq 2
, apply the second deduction.

•

If
a\neq 1,b=2
, apply the first deduction.

•

If
a=2
or
b=1
(or both), apply the third deduction, then the first or second as needed.

In the following, we show the deductions of the rest of the identities with less degree of detail. The computations are lengthy but straightforward to check.

(D2)

Identities with one
U
-monomial, with exponents of type
e
and 3 distinct subindices:

We assume
a,b,c,d\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
are pairwise different.

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{ba}^{2}}x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{ca}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{ac}^{2}}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ac}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{bc}^{2}}x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{ba}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{2}E_{ba}E_{ab}E_{ba}(*)}x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{cb}}

(4.5)

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-E_{da}E_{ad}(*)}x^{e_{db}}y^{e_{cd}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-E_{bd}}x^{e_{ad}}y^{e_{ca}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ac}}\xrightarrow{-E_{cb}}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ab}}

Let us show the fifth deduction of (
(D2)
) explicitly:

x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{E_{da}}x^{e_{db}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-E_{ad}}-x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}+x^{e_{db}}y^{e_{cd}};

adding
x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}
(which is the starting
U
-monomial of the deduction) we deduce
x^{e_{db}}y^{e_{cd}}
.

Now we explicit the fourth deduction:

x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-E_{ba}}x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\,\text{(a)}\xrightarrow{-E_{ab}}2x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}+x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{cb}};

subtracting
2x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}
(twice the starting
U
-monomial of the deduction) we deduce
x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{cb}}
and continue with

x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{cb}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{2}E_{ba}}x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{cb}}+\frac{1}{2}x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}};

subtracting now
\frac{1}{2}x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}
(which we deduced in (a)) we finally deduce
x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{cb}}
.

From (
(D3)
) we deduce that, starting from
x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{31}}\in J
, we can reach any
U
-monomial identity of the forms
x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}
,
x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ac}}
,
x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{ca}}
with
a,b,c\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
pairwise different, for any
k\geq 3
: deductions 1-4 show that we can get any valid permutation of the indices involved without introducing new ones (a crucial property for
k=3
), deductions 5-6 allow to change the indices to others not appearing in the original identity, and deduction 7 (together with deduction 2) shows that we can reach
x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{12}}
and thus all identities with only two distinct indices (by
(D1)
), whence
x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{12}}
is superfluous in the basis of identities for
k\geq 3
.

(D3)

Identities with one
U
-monomial, with exponents of type
e
and 4 distinct subindices:

We assume
a,b,c,d,i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
are pairwise different.

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ca}}\xrightarrow{-E_{ad}}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{ba}^{2}}x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{cd}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{dc}^{2}}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{dc}}

(4.6)

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{bc}^{2}}x^{e_{ac}}y^{e_{bd}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}\xrightarrow{E_{ia}}x^{e_{ib}}y^{e_{cd}}

From (
(D3)
) we deduce that, starting from
x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{31}}\in J
, we can reach any
U
-monomial identity of the form
x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{cd}}
with
a,b,c,d\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
all distinct: the first deduction allows to reach four distinct subindices, deductions 2-4 generate all permutations of the 4 subindices (through 3 transpositions) without the need of introducing new subindices (a crucial property for
k=4
), and the last deduction allows the introduction of new subindices.

Through
(D1)
,
(D2)
,
(D3)
we have shown that identity
1
of list (L) is in
J
.

(D4)

Identities with one
U
-monomial, with exponents of type
h
:

\displaystyle x^{e_{ii+1}}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{-E_{i+1i}}x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ab}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(a\neq i;b\neq i+1)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ii+1}}y^{e_{ai+1}}\xrightarrow{-E_{i+1i}}x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ai+1}}+x^{e_{ii+1}}y^{e_{ai}}\underset{J}{\equiv}x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ai+1}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(a\neq i,i+1)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ii+1}}\xrightarrow{-E_{i+1i}}x^{e_{ab}}y^{h_{i}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(a\neq i;b\neq i+1)$}}

(4.7)

\displaystyle x^{e_{ib}}y^{e_{ii+1}}\xrightarrow{E_{i+1i}}x^{e_{i+1b}}y^{e_{ii+1}}+x^{e_{ib}}y^{h_{i}}\underset{J}{\equiv}x^{e_{ib}}y^{h_{i}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(b\neq i,i+1)$}}

\displaystyle x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{jj+1}}\xrightarrow{-E_{j+1j}}x^{h_{i}}y^{h_{j}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(i\neq j-1,j,j+1)$}}

In the second and fourth deductions we have used
(D2)
.

From (
(D4)
) we deduce that, starting from
x^{e_{12}}y^{e_{31}}\in J
, we can reach any
U
-monomial identity of the forms
x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ab}}
(
a\neq i,i+1
),
x^{e_{ab}}y^{h_{i}}
(
b\neq i,i+1
),
x^{h_{i}}y^{h_{j}}
(
i\neq j-1,j,j+1
), with
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
: by
(D3)
we can reach the starting
U
-monomials of deductions 1-4 (with the corresponding restrictions on
a,b,i
), so we can reach their ending
U
-monomials; then the ending
U
-monomials of deductions 1-2 are the starting
U
-monomials of deduction 5. Therefore identities
2
,
3
,
4
of list (L) are in
J
.

(D5)

Auxiliary identities:

\displaystyle x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{li}}\xrightarrow{-E_{jl}}x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{li}}-x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{ji}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(l\neq j)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{il}}y^{h_{j}}\xrightarrow{-E_{lj}}x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j}}-x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(l\neq j+1)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ij+1}}y^{e_{jj+1}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{2}E_{j+1j}^{2}}x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j}}-x^{e_{ij+1}}y^{e_{j+1j}}

(4.8)

\displaystyle x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{lj}}\xrightarrow{E_{il}}x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ij}}-x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(l\neq i+1)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{i+1i}}y^{e_{i+1j}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{2}E_{ii+1}^{2}}x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ij}}-x^{e_{ii+1}}y^{e_{i+1j}}

Since
x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{li}}
is an identity in
J
for

(by
(D2)
), by deduction 1 we have

x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{li}}\underset{J}{\equiv}x^{e_{ij}}y^{e_{ji}}

for all valid
1\leq i,j,l\leq k
.
Since

(

) and

are identities in

(by

,

), by deductions 2-3 we get

for all valid
1\leq i,j,l\leq k
.
Analogously, from deductions 4-5, we get

x^{h_{i}}y^{e_{ij}}\underset{J}{\equiv}x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}.

(D6)

Identities in 2 or 3
U
-monomials with variables in the same order:

\displaystyle x^{e_{il}}y^{h_{j-1}}\xrightarrow{-E_{lj}}x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j-1}}+x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(l\neq j-1)$}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ij-1}}y^{e_{jj-1}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{j-1j}^{2}}x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j-1}}+x^{e_{ij-1}}y^{e_{j-1j}}\,\,

\displaystyle x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{lj}}\xrightarrow{E_{il}}x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}\,\,\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(l\neq i-1)$}}

(4.9)

\displaystyle x^{e_{i-1i}}y^{e_{i-1j}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{ii-1}^{2}}x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{ii-1}}y^{e_{i-1j}}\,\,

,

),(

\displaystyle x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j}}=(x^{h_{i-1}}y^{e_{ij}}+x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}})+(x^{e_{ij}}y^{h_{j}}-x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{lj}}).

Finally, starting from (

) particularized with

by (

). Hence identity

.

All identities in this subsection have been reached starting from

.

(D7)

Identities in 2
U
-monomials with permuted variables:

by the application of the following deductions, similar to those in

. We assume

are pairwise different:

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}\xrightarrow{-E_{ac}}x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{bc}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}\xrightarrow{E_{ca}}x^{e_{cb}}y^{e_{ba}}

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}\xrightarrow{-E_{ca}E_{ac}(*)}x^{e_{cb}}y^{e_{bc}}

(4.10)

\displaystyle x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}\xrightarrow{\frac{1}{4}E_{ba}E_{ab}E_{ba}E_{ab}(*)}x^{e_{ba}}y^{e_{ab}}

E.g., we reach

applying deduction 4 to

(by linearity), etc.
Let us explicit deduction 4:

x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}\xrightarrow{-E_{ba}}x^{h_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}\,\text{(a)}\xrightarrow{E_{ab}}-2x^{e_{ab}}y^{e_{ba}}+x^{h_{ab}}y^{h_{ab}};

-monomial of the deduction) we deduce

and continue with

(which we deduced in (a)) we deduce

and continue with

adding now
\frac{1}{2}x^{h_{ab}}y^{h_{ab}}
(which we deduced in (b)) we finally deduce

.

To reach

and

from auxiliary identities (
(D5)
),(

) (reached in

To reach

,

of list (L).

of list (L).

of list (L) we reach

). Therefore

by deductions (

).

In this subsection, we have shown that identity

is in

.

(D8)

Identities with brackets:

U
-polynomial
[x^{g},y^{g}]
is already a generator of

. To reach
[x^{g},y^{a}]
with

a,b,c\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
are pairwise different:

\displaystyle x^{g}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{E_{ca}}x^{g}y^{e_{cb}}

\displaystyle x^{g}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{-E_{bc}}x^{g}y^{e_{ac}}

\displaystyle x^{g}y^{e_{ab}}\xrightarrow{-\frac{1}{2}E_{ba}^{2}}x^{g}y^{e_{ba}}

(4.11)

\displaystyle x^{g}y^{e_{ii+1}}\xrightarrow{-E_{i+1i}}x^{g}y^{h_{i}}

In addition, by the previous subsection,

x^{h_{i-1}}y^{h_{i}}\underset{J}{\equiv}y^{h_{i-1}}x^{h_{i}}\equiv_{J}y^{h_{i}}x^{h_{i-1}},

since by identity (

) and identity (11) of the list (L) we get

x^{h_{i-1}}y^{h_{i}}\underset{J}{\equiv}-x^{e_{il}}y^{e_{li}}\underset{J}{\equiv}x^{h_{i}}y^{h_{i-1}}.

Therefore
[x^{h_{i-1}},y^{h_{i}}]\in J
. With this we have shown that identities

,
